Hybrid machine learning techniques for knowledge extraction in communication networks and advanced decision based complex q-Rung orthopair fuzzy frameworks
Authors: Hushuang Zeng, Yonghua Zhang
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
Hybrid machine learning techniques for knowledge extraction in communication networks combine the strengths of multiple learning algorithms to analyze complex, high-dimensional, and dynamic network data effectively. Some advanced approaches are discussed to improve the intelligence and efficiency of modern communication networks, making them more resilient and capable of meeting the growing demands of data-driven applications. This article articulates the efficiency and feasibility of a complex q-rung orthopair fuzzy set (Cq-ROFS), which is an extended version of an intuitionistic and q-rung orthopair fuzzy model. The Cq-ROFS has extensive information about any object in the form of amplitude and phase terms. Besides the theoretical concepts of Cq-ROFS, we modified the family of weighted average and weighted geometric operators using fundamental operations of Sugeno-Weber t-norms and t-conorms. An optimization technique of the WASPAS method is adopted to integrate the ranking of alternatives based on multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) problems. To show the validation and compatibility of diagnosed approaches, we also discussed an experimental case study to assess some advanced communication techniques using developed mathematical approaches and a decision analysis system. The sensitivity analysis and comparative study with existing mathematical methodologies are employed to demonstrate the flexibility and superiority of the discussed approaches.

Introduction

A decision-making problem involves selecting the best possible option from a set of alternatives based on specific criteria, often under conditions of uncertainty or conflicting objectives. These problems arise across various domains such as business, engineering, healthcare, and public policy, and dynamic environments complicate the evaluation process. A typical decision-making problem requires identifying relevant criteria, assigning appropriate weights to reflect their importance, and assessing how well each alternative satisfies these criteria. Challenges include handling subjective judgments, incomplete or imprecise information, and the need to balance qualitative and quantitative factors. It became difficult for decision-makers to solve decision-making problems with exact numerical values as a result of the increasing inherent uncertainties and ambiguities in data. Due to the imprecise nature of decision information, fuzzy sets (FS) theory has become increasingly prominent in recent years as a solution to decision-making problems.

In 1965, Zadeh1 initiated the theoretical concepts of FSs, which is a generalization of crisp sets to express vagueness and uncertainty in human opinions under closed interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left[\text{0,1}\right]$$\end{document}. The FS demonstrates only one component of an object in the form of a membership term (MT). Later on, Atanassov felt that human opinions were not unidirectional. Therefore, he developed a broader fuzzy framework of an intuitionistic fuzzy set (IFS)2. An IFS demonstrates two components of an object like MT and non-membership term (NMT) which is mathematically expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \overline{\overline{\mathcal{u}}}+\overline{\overline{\mathcal{v}}}\le 1$$\end{document}. Yager3 presented a modified fuzzy framework of pythagorean fuzzy set (PyFS) with relaxed mathematical conditions by taking the sum of the square of MT and NMT as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \overline{\overline{{\mathcal{u}}^{2}}}+\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{v}}^{2}}}\le 1$$\end{document}. Yager4 also designed a broader fuzzy framework of q-rung orthopair fuzzy models. The mathematical conditions of q-ROFS are given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \overline{\overline{{\mathcal{u}}^{q}}}+\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{v}}^{q}}}\le 1, q\ge 1$$\end{document}. A number of real-life applications resolved by employing the above-discussed fuzzy environments and decision-making models. Xu5 developed mathematical terminologies of IFSs to resolve multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) problems. Ayyildiz et al.6designed some novel decision-making techniques of AHP and WASPAS methods under the system of pythagorean fuzzy context. Abbas et al7. applied properties of entropy measures to classify renewable energy sources, taking into account pythagorean fuzzy models. Some robust mathematical aggregation operators and decision-making approaches were also developed by different scientists or research scholars8–10.

With the passage of time, Ramot et al11. introduced an extended fuzzy framework of complex fuzzy sets (CFS) considering two components of MT, amplitude term and phase term. Alkouri and Salleh12 designed a broader fuzzy framework of complex intuitionistic fuzzy sets (CIFS) by expressing amplitude and phase terms of MTs and NMTs, respectively. Alkouri and Salleh13also modified some robust operations of CIFSs to cope with uncertain human opinions. Ullah et al14. illustrated the theoretical concepts of distance measures using complex PyFSs. Ali and Mahmood15 discussed the properties of Maclaurin symmetric mean operators using complex q-ROFSs.

Literature review

Fuzzy aggregation operators play a crucial role in decision-making problems by enabling the combination of uncertain, imprecise, or subjective information from multiple criteria or experts into a single representative value. Traditional crisp aggregation techniques often fail to capture the vagueness inherent in human judgments or qualitative assessments. Xu16 designed some advanced arithmetic operators under the system of intuitionistic fuzzy models. Akram and Ali17 illustrated hybrid decision analysis models to integrate human opinion under different criteria. Garg18developed new approaches to entropy measures to investigate weights of criteria under the system of intuitionistic fuzzy framework. Ali et al19. presented Heronian mean aggregation operators to resolve conflicting criteria in decision-making problems. Duc Trung20 put forward a novel approach of an efficient decision-making approach using the MARCOS method.

Akram and Bibi21initiated mathematical approaches to aggregate a group of expert’s opinions using the decision-making model of the PROMETHEE method. Ali et al22. applied the operational laws of Aczel Alsina t-norm and t-conorm, taking into account an intuitionistic fuzzy soft context. Hussain et al23. investigated some feasible renewable energy sources using mathematical approaches of Sugeno-Weber aggregation operators. Nazir et al24. modified Hamy mean models using circular pythagorean fuzzy information to analyse advanced petroleum exploration techniques. Rukhsar et al25. resolved complicated real-life applications of green supplier selection using a decision analysis model. Hussain et al26. evaluated different renewable energy sources using a group of expert’s opinions and decision analysis systems. Hussain et al27. examined different digital security approaches based on specific criteria and Sugeno-Weber mathematical approaches. Ahmed et al28. established a novel approach to the decision-making model by incorporating a complex intuitionistic fuzzy framework. Akram et al29. developed Yager aggregation operators to handle uncertainty during the aggregation process of expert judgments. Hussain et al30. developed Dombi Bonferroni mean operators, taking into account the picture fuzzy framework and decision-making models. Ali et al31. presented power Aczel Alsina aggregation models to investigate unknown degrees of weights and resolve insufficient information of human opinions. Farid et al32. modified a list of Schweizer-Sklar power aggregation operators to assess advanced environmentally-conscious technologies for road freight delivery. Hussain et al33. evaluated construction materials using Hamy mean operators and an advanced decision support system. Gurmani et al34. utilized properties of algebraic operations, taking into account linguistic t-spherical fuzzy framework and decision-making problems. Abosuliman et al35. investigated suitable nanomaterials for Nanosensors using theoretical concepts of fuzzy neural networks and decision-making models. Alballa et al36. established a reliable optimization technique using Frank aggregation operators and a multi-criteria decision-making approach. Gurmani et al37. resolved an application related to emergency medical suppliers. Ramya et al38. discovered a feasible plan to recycle usable plastic materials by applying advanced technologies and mathematical approaches. Tariq et al39. employed smart artificial intelligence techniques to increase the capacity of electricity in educational institutes. A robust family of Heronian mean operators and a decision-making model were discussed in40. Rahman and Muhammad41 derived mathematical approaches using different properties of confidence level, taking into account complex polytopic fuzzy situations and decision-making models. Rahman42presented an overview of nuclear power plants employing the system of complex polytopic fuzzy environment and confidence level-based aggregation operators. Sen et al43. discussed key features of wireless sensor networks by applying Einstein aggregation operators with a decision analysis system. Hussain and Alam44 initiated some reliable detection methods for road cracks and segmentation under Einstein aggregation operators. Riaz and Farid45 enhanced a theoretical overview of linear Diophantine fuzzy systems with decision-making models. Sarfraz and Božanić46developed feasible Maclaurin symmetric mean models to investigate risk factors in the market. Latif et al47. discussed Prioritized Aczel Alsina aggregation operators under consideration of single valued Neutrosophic numbers. Some appropriate mathematical models and decision analysis systems also mentioned in48–50.

Research gaps

The CqROF framework is an advanced extension of traditional fuzzy set theories designed to handle higher degrees of uncertainty and imprecision in complex decision-making scenarios. It generalizes intuitionistic and pythagorean FSs by allowing the sum of the q-th powers of membership and non-membership degrees to be less than or equal to one, and it further incorporates phase terms under a complex plan. This allows the CqROF model to better capture ambiguity in data where both amplitude and phase are relevant. Within this framework, Sugeno-Weber (SW) aggregation operators play a crucial role by combining multiple CqROF values using parameterized functions that account for interaction among criteria. These operators provide flexible and robust tools for aggregating complex fuzzy information, preserving the uncertainty and interdependence among inputs while enabling comprehensive evaluations in multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) problems. Keeping in mind the significance of Sugeno-Weber aggregation operators51–53, we developed a family of robust mathematical methodologies under consideration.Cq-ROF framework.

Contributions of this study

The main contributions of the manuscript are listed as follows:To apply a novel approach of the Cq-ROF framework to integrate uncertain and imprecise information of expert judgments during the decision analysis process.Some flexible operations of Sugeno-Weber triangular norms are formulated under consideration of Cq-ROF frameworks.We derive a family of mathematical approaches of Sugeno-Weber weighted average and weighted geometric operators under consideration of Cq-ROF frameworks.By incorporating the modified SW operators into the WASPAS method, the proposed model benefits from the strengths of both weighted sum and weighted product approaches, leading to improved ranking accuracy and robustness in complex decision-making scenarios.We also demonstrate the practical applicability of the proposed methodology through a real-world case study, showing its effectiveness in handling complex decision-making environments with uncertain and periodic information.A comparative analysis with existing methods and a sensitivity analysis of key parameters are conducted, proving the reliability and superiority of the proposed hybrid approach over conventional techniques.

Organization of the study

The remaining sections of the manuscript are expressed as follows: Sect. “Basic concepts” illustrates basic notions of Sugeno-Weber t-norms and t-conorm, fundamental operations, as well as the notion of Cq-ROFSs. Sect. “Fundamental Operations of Sugeno-Weber Triangular Norms” formulates some feasible operations of Sugeno-Weber triangular norms. A list of mathematical approaches to Sugeno-Weber weighted average operators is derived in Sect. “Complex q-Rung Orthopair Sugeno-Weber Averaging Aggregation Operators”. We also deduced a family of geometric aggregation operators using operations of Sugeno-Weber triangular norms in Sect. “Complex q-Rung Orthopair Sugeno-Weber Geometric Aggregation Operators”. Sect. “Decision-Making Methodologies” designed a novel approach to the WASPAS method employing mathematical approaches of Sugeno-Weber t-norms. To show the validation and applicability of the proposed mathematical models, we establish a sensitivity analysis using derived mathematical methodologies in Sect. Sensitivity Analysis”. Sect. “Comparison Method” demonstrates a comprehensive comparison method with existing approaches. At the end, a summary of the manuscript is presented in Sect. “Conclusions”. Figure 1 also elaborates key outlines and primary contributions of the article.Fig. 1Diagram explores key contributions of the article.

Basic concepts

Here, we explore notions of Sugeno-Weber t-norm and t-conorm, Cq-ROFSs with some fundamental operations for the development of new approaches.

Definition 1:

Ref52 The theory of Sugeno-Weber t-norm and t-conorm is expressed as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}^{\lambda }\left(\mathcal{s}, \mathcal{p}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}{c}\begin{array}{cc}{T}_{D}\left(\mathcal{s}, \mathcal{p}\right)& if \lambda =-1\end{array}\\ \begin{array}{cc}max\left(0,\frac{\mathcal{s}+ \mathcal{p}-1+\lambda \mathcal{s}\mathcal{p}}{1+\lambda }\right)& if -1<\lambda <+\infty \end{array}\\ \begin{array}{cc}{T}_{P}\left(\mathcal{s}, \mathcal{p}\right)& \lambda =+\infty \end{array}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^{\lambda } (s,p) = \left\{ {\min } \right.(1,\begin{array}{*{20}c} {S_{D} (s,p)\,if\lambda = - 1} \\ {s + p - \frac{\lambda }{1 + \lambda }sp)} \\ {S_{p} (s,p)\,\lambda = + \infty } \\ \end{array} if - 1 < \lambda < + \infty$$\end{document}

Definition 4:

Ref54 For three q-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta =\left(\alpha , \gamma \right), {\zeta }_{1}=\left({\alpha }_{1}, {\gamma }_{1}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{2}=\left({\alpha }_{2}, {\gamma }_{2}\right)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta>0$$\end{document}. Then, we have:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{1)}\:\:\:\:\zeta_{1} \oplus \zeta_{2} = \left( {\sqrt[a]{{\alpha^{q}_{1} + \alpha^{q}_{2} - \alpha^{q}_{1} \,\alpha^{q}_{2} ,\gamma_{1} \,\gamma_{2} }}} \right)$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{2)}\:\:\:\:\zeta_{1} \otimes \zeta_{2} = \left( {\alpha_{1} \,\alpha_{2} ,\sqrt[q]{{\gamma_{1}^{q} + \gamma_{2}^{q} - \gamma_{1}^{q} \,\gamma_{2}^{q} }}} \right)$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{3)}\:\:\:\:\Delta \zeta = \left( {\sqrt[q]{{1 - (1 - \alpha^{q} )^{\Delta } ,\gamma^{\Delta } }}} \right)$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{4)}\:\:\:\:\zeta^{\Delta } = \left( {\alpha^{\Delta } ,\sqrt[q]{{1 - (1 - \gamma^{q} )^{\Delta } }}} \right)$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{1)}\:\:\:\:\zeta_{1} \oplus \zeta_{2} = \left( {\sqrt[a]{{\alpha^{q}_{1} + \alpha^{q}_{2} - \alpha^{q}_{1} \,\alpha^{q}_{2} ,\gamma_{1} \,\gamma_{2} }}} \right)$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{1)}\:\:\:\:\zeta_{1} \oplus \zeta_{2} = \left( {\sqrt[a]{{\alpha^{q}_{1} + \alpha^{q}_{2} - \alpha^{q}_{1} \,\alpha^{q}_{2} ,\gamma_{1} \,\gamma_{2} }}} \right)$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{2)}\:\:\:\:\zeta_{1} \otimes \zeta_{2} = \left( {\alpha_{1} \,\alpha_{2} ,\sqrt[q]{{\gamma_{1}^{q} + \gamma_{2}^{q} - \gamma_{1}^{q} \,\gamma_{2}^{q} }}} \right)$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{2)}\:\:\:\:\zeta_{1} \otimes \zeta_{2} = \left( {\alpha_{1} \,\alpha_{2} ,\sqrt[q]{{\gamma_{1}^{q} + \gamma_{2}^{q} - \gamma_{1}^{q} \,\gamma_{2}^{q} }}} \right)$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text{3)}\:\:\:\:\Delta \zeta = \left( {\sqrt[q]{{1 - (1 - \alpha^{q} )^{\Delta } ,\gamma^{\Delta } }}} \right)$$\end{document}

Definition 5:

Ref55 Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W$$\end{document} be a source set and Cq-ROFS \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A$$\end{document} is articulate as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\left\{\sigma , \left(\alpha \left(\sigma \right), \gamma \left(\sigma \right)\right)|\sigma \in W\right\}$$\end{document}

Note that MT \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =\mathcal{u}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\psi \right)}\text{ and } and$$\end{document}NMT \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =\mathcal{v}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\varphi \right)}$$\end{document} partitioned into two components: amplitude and phase terms. The Cq-ROFS satisfies the following conditions:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le {\mathcal{u}}^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\sigma \right)+{\mathcal{v}}^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\sigma \right)\le 1\text{ and }0\le {\psi }^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\sigma \right)+{\varphi }^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\sigma \right)\le 1, \mathsf{q}\in {\mathbb{Z}}^{+}$$\end{document}

The indeterminacy value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma$$\end{document} in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A$$\end{document} is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r\left(\sigma \right)=\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{1-\left({\mathcal{u}}^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\sigma \right)+{\mathcal{v}}^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\sigma \right)\right)}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{1-\left({\psi }^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\sigma \right)+{\varphi }^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\sigma \right)\right)}\right)}$$\end{document}. A complex q-rung orthopair fuzzy value (q-ROFV) is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta =\left(\mathcal{u}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\psi \right)},\mathcal{v}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\varphi \right)}\right)$$\end{document}.

Definition 6:

Ref55 If Cq-ROFV \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta =\left(\mathcal{u}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\psi \right)},\mathcal{v}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\varphi \right)}\right)$$\end{document}. Then, the score and accuracy functions are given as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}}\left(\zeta \right)=\frac{1}{4}\left(\left(1+\mathcal{u}-\mathcal{v}\right)+\left(1+\psi -\varphi \right)\right), {\mathbb{S}}\left(\zeta \right)\in \left[\text{0,1}\right]$$\end{document}

and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{A}}\left(\zeta \right)=\frac{1}{4}\left(\mathcal{u}+\mathcal{v}+\psi +\varphi \right), {\mathbb{A}}\left(\zeta \right)\in \left[\text{0,1}\right]$$\end{document}

Fundamental operations of sugeno-weber triangular norms

In this section, we formulate some feasible operations of Sugeno-Weber t-norm and t-conorms under consideration of Cq-ROF information.

Definition 7:

Complex q-Rung orthopair sugeno-weber averaging aggregation operators

We developed new mathematical approaches of Sugeno-Weber weighted average operators with appropriate properties.

Definition 8:

Let Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document}, and the Cq-ROFSWA operator is characterized as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Cq - ROFSWWA(\zeta_{1} ,\zeta_{2} , \ldots ,\zeta_{n} ) = \mathop \oplus \limits_{i = 1}^{n} w_{i} \zeta_{i}$$\end{document}

Theorem 1:

Let Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document}. The estimated value by the Cq-ROFSWWA operator is also a Cq-ROFV as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Cq-ROFSWWA\left({\zeta }_{1}, {\zeta }_{2}, \dots , {\zeta }_{n}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1+\lambda }{\lambda }\left(1-\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\left(1-{\mathcal{u}}_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{\lambda }{1+\lambda }\right)\right)}^{{\mathcal{w}}_{i}}\right)}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1+\lambda }{\mathsf{q}\lambda }\left(1-\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\left(1-{\psi }_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{\lambda }{1+\lambda }\right)\right)}^{{\mathcal{w}}_{i}}\right)}\right)},\\ \sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1}{\lambda }\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\lambda \right){\left(\frac{\lambda {\mathcal{v}}_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}+1}{1+\lambda }\right)}^{{\mathcal{w}}_{i}}-1\right)}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1}{\lambda }\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\lambda \right){\left(\frac{\lambda {\varphi }_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}+1}{1+\lambda }\right)}^{{\mathcal{w}}_{i}}-1\right)}\right)}\end{array}\right)$$\end{document}

Theorem 2:

Let Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document} implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\zeta$$\end{document}. Then we have:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Cq-ROFSWWA\left({\zeta }_{1}, {\zeta }_{2}, \dots , {\zeta }_{n}\right)=\zeta$$\end{document}

Proof:

Since all Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document} are identical \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\varvec{\zeta}}}_{{\varvec{i}}}={\varvec{\zeta}}$$\end{document}. Then we have:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Cq-ROFSWWA\left({\zeta }_{1}, {\zeta }_{2}, \dots , {\zeta }_{n}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1+\lambda }{\lambda }\left(1-\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\left(1-{\psi }_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{\lambda }{1+\lambda }\right)\right)}^{{\mathcal{w}}_{i}}\right)}\right)},\\ \sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1}{\lambda }\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\lambda \right){\left(\frac{\lambda {\mathcal{v}}_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}+1}{1+\lambda }\right)}^{{\mathcal{w}}_{i}}-1\right)}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1}{\lambda }\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\lambda \right){\left(\frac{\lambda {\varphi }_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}+1}{1+\lambda \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1+\lambda }{\lambda }\left(1-\left(1-{\mathcal{u}}^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{\lambda }{1+\lambda }\right)\right)\right)}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1+\lambda }{\lambda }\left(1-\left(1-{\psi }^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{\lambda }{1+\lambda }\right)\right)\right)}\right)},\\ \sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1}{\lambda }\left(\left(1+\lambda \right)\left(\frac{\lambda {\mathcal{v}}^{\mathsf{q}}+1}{1+\lambda }\right)-1\right)}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1}{\lambda }\left(\left(1+\lambda \right)\left(\frac{\lambda {\varphi }^{\mathsf{q}}+1}{1+\lambda }\right)-1\right)}\right)}\end{array}\right)$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$= \left( {\begin{array}{*{20}c} {\sqrt[q]{{\frac{1 + \lambda }{\lambda }\left( {1 - Theorem 3:

For any two sets of a Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}{\prime}=\left({{\mathcal{u}}{\prime}}_{i}, {\Pi }_{i}{\prime}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{\prime}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document}. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}\le {\zeta }_{i}{\prime}$$\end{document}, so we have:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Cq-ROFSWWA\left({\zeta }_{1}, {\zeta }_{2}, \dots , {\zeta }_{n}\right)\le Cq-ROFSWWA\left({\zeta }_{1}{\prime}, {\zeta }_{2}{\prime}, \dots , {\zeta }_{n}{\prime}\right)$$\end{document}

Theorem 4:

Let Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document}. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }^{-}=\left(\text{min}\left\{{\mathcal{u}}_{i}\right\}, \text{max}\left\{{\mathcal{v}}_{i}\right\}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }^{+}=\left(\text{max}\left\{{\mathcal{u}}_{i}\right\}, \text{min}\left\{{\mathcal{v}}_{i}\right\}\right)$$\end{document}. Then we have:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }^{-}\le Cq-ROFSWWA\left({\zeta }_{1},\boldsymbol{ }{\zeta }_{2},\boldsymbol{ }\dots ,\boldsymbol{ }{\zeta }_{n}\right)\le {\zeta }^{+}$$\end{document}

Complex q-Rung orthopair sugeno-weber geometric aggregation operators

We combined theoretical concepts of weighted geometric operators and Sugeno-Weber triangular norms.

Definition 9:

Let Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document}, and the Cq-ROFSWWG operator is characterized as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Cq - ROFSWWG(\zeta_{1} ,\zeta_{2} , \ldots ,\zeta_{n} ) = \mathop \otimes \limits_{i = 1}^{n} \zeta_{i}^{{w_{i} }}$$\end{document}

Theorem 5:

Let Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document}. The estimated value by the Cq-ROFSWWG operator is also a Cq-ROFV as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Cq-ROFSWWG\left({\zeta }_{1}, {\zeta }_{2}, \dots , {\zeta }_{n}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{c}\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1}{\lambda }\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\lambda \right){\left(\frac{\lambda {\mathcal{u}}_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}+1}{1+\lambda }\right)}^{{\mathcal{w}}_{i}}-1\right)}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1}{\lambda }\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\lambda \right){\left(\frac{\lambda {\psi }_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}+1}{1+\lambda }\right)}^{{\mathcal{w}}_{i}}-1\right)}\right)},\\ \sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1+\lambda }{\lambda }\left(1-\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\left(1-{\mathcal{v}}_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{\lambda }{1+\lambda }\right)\right)}^{{\mathcal{w}}_{i}}\right)}{e}^{2\pi i\left(\sqrt[\mathsf{q}]{\frac{1+\lambda }{\lambda }\left(1-\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\left(1-{\varphi }_{i}^{\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{\lambda }{1+\lambda }\right)\right)}^{{\mathcal{w}}_{i}}\right)}\right)}\end{array}\right)$$\end{document}

Theorem 6:

Let Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document} implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\zeta$$\end{document}. Then we have:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Cq-ROFSWWG\left({\zeta }_{1}, {\zeta }_{2}, \dots , {\zeta }_{n}\right)=\zeta$$\end{document}

Theorem 7:

For any two sets of a Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}{\prime}=\left({{\mathcal{u}}{\prime}}_{i}, {\Pi }_{i}{\prime}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{\prime}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document}. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}\le {\zeta }_{i}{\prime}$$\end{document}, so we have:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Cq-ROFSWWG\left({\zeta }_{1}, {\zeta }_{2}, \dots , {\zeta }_{n}\right)\le Cq-ROFSWWG\left({\zeta }_{1}{\prime}, {\zeta }_{2}{\prime}, \dots , {\zeta }_{n}{\prime}\right)$$\end{document}

Theorem 8:

Let Cq-ROFVs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{i}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{i}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{i}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{i}\right)}\right),i=1, 2, \dots , n$$\end{document}. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }^{-}=\left(\text{min}\left\{{\mathcal{u}}_{i}\right\}, \text{max}\left\{{\mathcal{v}}_{i}\right\}\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }^{+}=\left(\text{max}\left\{{\mathcal{u}}_{i}\right\}, \text{min}\left\{{\mathcal{v}}_{i}\right\}\right)$$\end{document}. Then we have:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\varvec{\zeta}}}^{-}\le {\varvec{C}}{\varvec{q}}-{\varvec{R}}{\varvec{O}}{\varvec{F}}{\varvec{S}}{\varvec{W}}{\varvec{W}}{\varvec{G}}\left({{\varvec{\zeta}}}_{1},\boldsymbol{ }{{\varvec{\zeta}}}_{2},\boldsymbol{ }\dots ,\boldsymbol{ }{{\varvec{\zeta}}}_{{\varvec{n}}}\right)\le {{\varvec{\zeta}}}^{+}$$\end{document}

Decision-making methodologies

In situations where several factors must be taken into account at once, the MADM’s involvement in choosing the best and most appropriate choice is crucial. Decision-makers frequently have to choose between a number of options in real-world scenarios, each of which is assessed according to a number of criteria, including cost, effectiveness, risk, and environmental impact. A systematic framework for weighing these traits and analyzing their trade-offs is offered by MADM. Decision-makers can rank options and determine which one best fits the overall goals by using MADM, which quantifies the significance of each criterion. We investigated an algorithm for MADM problems employing our suggested methods, such as Cq-ROFSWWA and Cq-ROFSWWG operators, in order to choose the desired optimal alternative.

Let a class of finite preferences or alternative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\left({A}_{1}, {A}_{2}, \dots , {A}_{m}\right)$$\end{document} are evaluated under a set of attributes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=\left({E}_{1},{E}_{2}, \dots , {E}_{n}\right)$$\end{document}. Furthermore, a family of the weight of criteria \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{w}=\left({\mathcal{w}}_{1}, {\mathcal{w}}_{2}, \dots , {\mathcal{w}}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{w}}_{i}>0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{w}}_{i}=1$$\end{document}. The decision-maker arranges expert’s judgments under consideration of Cq-ROF information as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{ij}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{ij}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{ij}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{ij}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{ij}\right)}\right)$$\end{document} with restricted conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le {\mathcal{u}}_{ij}^{\mathsf{q}}+{\mathcal{v}}_{ij}^{\mathsf{q}}\le 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le {\psi }_{ij}^{\mathsf{q}}+{\varphi }_{ij}^{\mathsf{q}}\le 1$$\end{document}. A Decision matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R={\left({\zeta }_{ij}\right)}_{m\times n}={\left({\mathcal{u}}_{ij}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{ij}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{ij}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{ij}\right)}\right)}_{m\times n}$$\end{document} contains information about various attributes corresponding to each alternative. There are two different decision-making models of the WASPAS method and the MCDM problem under consideration in the Cq-ROF framework. Step 2: Obtained a transform decision matrix based on the following expression:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{ij}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{ij}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{ij}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{ij}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{ij}\right)}\right), i,j=\text{1,2},\dots , n,m$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{ij}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{ij}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{ij}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{ij}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{ij}\right)}\right), i,j=\text{1,2},\dots , n,m$$\end{document}

Orthonormal decision-matrix investigated based on transform decision-matrix as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\zeta }_{ij}{\prime}=\left({\mathcal{u}}_{ij}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\psi }_{ij}\right)}, {\mathcal{v}}_{ij}{e}^{2\pi i\left({\varphi }_{ij}\right)}\right)= \left\{\begin{array}{c}0 otherwise\\ \frac{{\mathcal{u}}_{ij}}{1+{\mathcal{u}}_{ij}} of {\mathcal{u}}_{ij}\le {\mathcal{u}}_{ij} \\ \frac{{\mathcal{v}}_{ij}}{1+{\mathcal{v}}_{ij}} for non-membership\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

B. Numerical example

B. Numerical example

Advanced hybrid machine-learning techniques have emerged as powerful tools for knowledge extraction in communication networks, addressing the increasing complexity and dynamism of modern network environments by integrating multiple machine-learning paradigms, such as combining supervised and reinforcement learning. These hybrid models capitalize on the strengths of each approach to improve accuracy and learning efficiency. In communication networks, they are applied to a wide range of tasks, including traffic prediction, intelligent routing, and intrusion detection. These techniques enable real-time analysis of massive and heterogeneous data generated by network devices, uncovering hidden patterns and insights that support proactive and autonomous network management.

In the experimental case, we evaluated different advanced ML approaches for the improvement of communication networks. However, there are five different approaches \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{{\text{O}}_{1}, {\text{O}}_{2}, {\text{O}}_{3}, {\text{O}}_{4}, {\text{O}}_{5}\right\}$$\end{document} consideration and evaluated under the following specific criteria.

Improved Accuracy and Prediction Performance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R}_{1}$$\end{document}

Multimodal Data Integration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R}_{2}$$\end{document}

Better Fault Detection and Diagnosis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R}_{3}$$\end{document}

Support for Proactive Network Management \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R}_{4}$$\end{document}

Security Enhancement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R}_{5}$$\end{document}

Improved User Quality of Experience \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R}_{6}$$\end{document}

MADM problem

We also applied the discussed decision algorithm of the MADM problem as follows:

Step 1: We considered the expert judgments in Table 1.

Step 2: Since all the discussed criteria in the experimental case study are of the same type. Therefore, we don’t need to transform the standard decision matrix into a normalized decision matrix.

Results of preferences.

In order to understand the structural behavior of aggregated results and obtained preferences by the MCDM problem and WASPAS method, we explored the results of alternatives in Fig. 3.Fig. 3Demonstrates results of decision-making methodologies.

Demonstrates results of decision-making methodologies.

Sensitivity analysis

To validate the robustness and reliability of the proposed decision-making model, a sensitivity analysis is conducted by varying the different parametric values of the Sugeno-Weber aggregation operators. The SW operators rely on an interaction parameter, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda$$\end{document}, which governs the degree of interaction or compensation between the aggregated criteria. By adjusting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda$$\end{document}, we can evaluate how sensitive the final rankings of alternatives are to changes in the aggregation behavior. Table 7 illustrates the results of the Cq-ROFSWWA and Cq-ROFSWWG operators by applying different parametric variables of Sugeno-Weber aggregation operators. Figures 4 and 5 also elaborate on the obtained results by the Cq-ROFSWWA and Cq-ROFSWWG operators.Table 7Decision matrix of Cq-ROF information.Results of Cq-ROFSWWA operators at different parametric However, slight variations are observed in the mid-ranked alternatives, which reflect the sensitivity of those options to interaction effects among criteria. This study confirms that the proposed model of Sugeno-Weber aggregation operators and the decision-making techniques of the WASPAS method are flexible and resilient in handling varying degrees of interaction among criteria.

Comparison method

This section is conducted to show the superiority and validation of the proposed operators using existing mathematical terminologies and decision-making methods. However, many mathematicians and research scholars have derived numerous aggregation operators that exist in the literature. The comparison method has great significance in the aggregation process and decision-making models. To achieve the main goals of this section, we considered mathematical approaches of different research scholars, such as Du et al56. developed Frank mathematical models to integrate human opinions during the aggregation process. Ahmmad et al57. modified the theoretical concepts of Yager t-norm and t-conorm to derive robust aggregation operators under the discussed fuzzy frameworks. Hussain et al58. put forward the theory of Muirhead mean operators to investigate the ranking of alternatives based on estimated conflicting criteria. Farid and Riaz59 modified Aczel Alsina operators to integrate human judgments by incorporating the q-rung orthopair fuzzy framework. Farid and Riaz60modified the attractive operational and Einstein t-norm and t-conorm to propose mathematical operators for resolving complexities and reasoning with data of human opinions. Riaz et al61. discussed an application related to low-carbon green supply chain enterprises employing q-rung orthopair fuzzy mathematical approaches. In Table 8, we can analyze that some existing mathematical terminologies are unable to handle expert opinions due to their limited structure and restricted fuzzy framework. However, derived aggregation operators and decision-making terminologies are superior to existing aggregation operators.

The WASPAS method and complex q-rung orthopair fuzzy Sugeno-Weber aggregation operators demonstrate significant superiority over existing decision-making approaches due to their enhanced ability to handle uncertainty, ambiguity, and interrelationships among criteria. The WASPAS method, which combines the strengths of both the WSA and WPA, offers a more balanced and accurate assessment of alternatives by leveraging both additive and multiplicative strategies. When integrated with Cq-ROF Sugeno-Weber operators, the model further benefits from a more flexible and robust representation of fuzzy information, especially in scenarios involving imprecise or conflicting data. The complex q-rung orthopair fuzzy environment extends the expressive capacity beyond traditional fuzzy and intuitionistic sets, while the Sugeno-Weber aggregation captures interaction and compensation effects among criteria.

Application of proposed work in other fields

The proposed hybrid machine learning model, combined with complex q-Rung orthopair fuzzy frameworks, shows extreme flexibility and a high degree of adaptability, and it can be used in various applications in the future. Other than communication networks, this way of modeling finds valuable use in areas such as the on-demand planning of aircraft slots using tree-structured capacity profiling and dynamic allocation mechanisms62, decentral pivoting path planning to self-reconfigurable satellites63, and solidification microstructure in cutting-edge welding procedures like K-TIG64, and magnetically-imparted arc butt welding65. Also, it has a future in the use of human-based modeling, like the determination of whether nighttime car body lights cause distraction in pedestrians through the utilization of virtual reality methods66, classification of renewable energy sources67, and reducing the impact of solid waste materials68.

Conclusions

In conclusion, this article presents a comprehensive and robust decision-making framework by integrating Cq-ROFSs with Sugeno-Weber weighted average and weighted geometric aggregation operators within the WASPAS method to effectively address MCDM problems. The use of complex q-rung orthopair fuzzy sets enhances the model’s capability to capture uncertainty in expert evaluations, providing a more expressive and accurate representation of real-world scenarios. The incorporation of Sugeno-Weber aggregation operators enables flexible and reliable fusion of fuzzy information. This article developed some flexible mathematical approaches of Cq-ROFSWWA and Cq-ROFSWWG operators with reliable properties. Furthermore, the integration with the WASPAS method combines the strengths of both WSA and WPA, leading to more accurate and stable rankings of alternatives. We also discussed an appropriate decision algorithm for the MCDM problem for solving complicated real-life applications. An experimental case study is also discussed to investigate some advanced communication techniques under specific criteria. The sensitivity analysis also captured the reliability of pioneering approaches and decision-making methodologies.

Although the suggested framework for decision-making is useful, it is important to recognize that the WASPAS technique and the use of sophisticated Cq-ROFSs have a number of drawbacks. In extremely non-linear decision spaces, the WASPAS may lose some of its effectiveness, and the final ranking results may be affected. However, the Cq-ROFS framework is effective in managing periodic information and uncertainty. Its practical relevance may be limited if decision-makers who are not conversant with sophisticated financial theories find it difficult to evaluate complex-valued membership and non-membership degrees.

To overcome these constraints, future studies can investigate data-driven or adaptive weight determination strategies to increase the WASPAS method’s adaptability. Combining the existing model with more sophisticated MCDM techniques like TOPSIS69, VIKOR70, or DEMATEL71, EDAS72 methods.
